Yes, I do think it’s intentional. I mean, it’s not just family disagreements. They use the altar, the church things, the parish committee—people listen to that, you know? And then suddenly I’m the one pushed aside, like all the years I’ve done things for this family don’t count.

My sister-in-law always knew how to make people look at her like she’s the good one… She did that even years ago at family gatherings, with the food and who got thanked and who didn’t. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My in-laws, mostly. I mean, that’s where it starts. And then the parish committee people, the ones connected with the altar and the church things—they may smile and act respectful, but they’re part of it too, or at least they’re letting themselves be used.

It’s hard to name every person because they don’t come out and say it plainly, you know? It’s in how they arrange things, who they thank, who they leave out. My sister-in-law especially has always been good at that—making herself look proper while I’m made to look like nothing. And now with the church involved, it feels like they’re trying to push me out completely, like all I’ve done for the family should just be forgotten.

I think it’s planned. I mean, maybe not everyone sits in one room and says it out loud like that, but it’s too organized to be just accidents. And now with the church involved, it feels like they’re trying to push me out completely, like all I’ve done for the family should just be forgotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these people you mentioned are coordinating their actions in a planned or organized way, or do you think they’re just acting on their own but in a way that ends up working against you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s planned. I mean, maybe not everyone sits in one room and says it out loud like that, but it’s too organized to be just accidents. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that church respect, to push me out.\n\nAnd you know, in families people always remember who brought what dish or who helped with the children, but suddenly with me it’s like none of it happened. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
One example is at the church, with the altar and the parish committee. They act like it’s just church business, you know, flowers here, names there, who gets thanked, who stands where—but it’s not just that. I could see how they were using it to push me out.

And my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. They got those church people involved, or let them be involved, so everyone would stop remembering what I’ve done for this family. All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.

It’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.

All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.\n\nIt’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.

I came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.

That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me, they were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.

I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.

So yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.

That’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.

My husband started staying close because I was missing meals, forgetting routines, and not caring for myself reliably.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole crisis has been going on for about two months, since the fight with my in-laws over the Día de los Muertos altar.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The heavy sadness and guilt have been with me for about nine months, though they became much harder to handle after the family conflict.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.

But it feels coordinated. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. Whether they sat in a room and planned it or just all know what they’re doing without saying it straight out… the result is the same. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.

They use the altar, the church things, the parish committee—people listen to that, you know? And the committee people, they act polite, but I can tell. My sister-in-law always knew how to make people look at her like she’s the good one… She did that even years ago at family gatherings, with the food and who got thanked and who didn’t. But this is bigger now. It feels organized. They’re trying to make people think less of me, push me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws have been deliberately conspiring or planning against you in a way that goes beyond normal family conflict or disagreement?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that’s what I’m saying. It’s not just normal arguing, not like when families bicker over food or who didn’t visit who. I came in because I knew my in-laws were doing more than that—using the altar, the parish committee, all those church people—to push me out.\n\nAnd it’s like they want everyone to forget what I’ve done for this family. I’ve spent years doing things, showing up, helping, and now suddenly it’s like I’m being erased. They act like it’s nothing, but I can see the planning in it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me exactly who you believe is involved in this effort to push you out and make people think less of you?\n\nPatient: My in-laws, mostly. I mean, that’s where it starts. And then the parish committee people, the ones connected with the altar and the church things—they may smile and act respectful, but they’re part of it too, or at least they’re letting themselves be used.\n\nIt’s hard to name every person because they don’t come out and say it plainly, you know? It’s in how they arrange things, who they thank, who they leave out. My sister-in-law especially has always been good at that—making herself look proper while I’m made to look like nothing. All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.

And you know, families can be petty—my aunt once stopped speaking to someone over a missing casserole dish for years—but this isn’t that. This has the church wrapped around it, so it looks official, like I’m the one who doesn’t belong. Whether it’s some big organized thing or just them all lining up together, it feels like an effort to erase me.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20, F60\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because things at home aren’t right. My in-laws, they’re not just arguing with me like people think. It’s more than that. They’ve been using the altar, and the parish committee too, to push me out… like they want everyone to forget everything I’ve done for this family. I know how that sounds, but I can see what they’re doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean when you say your in-laws are using the altar and the parish committee against you? Do you believe they are intentionally trying to harm you or control what others think of you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it’s intentional. I mean, it’s not just family disagreements. They use the altar, the church things, the parish committee—people listen to that, you know? And then suddenly I’m the one pushed aside, like all the years I’ve done things for this family don’t count.\n\nIt’s like they want everyone to forget me, forget what I’ve done. And the committee people, they act polite, but I can tell. My sister-in-law always knew how to make people look at her like she’s the good one… She did that even years ago at family gatherings, with the food and who got thanked and who didn’t. But this is bigger now. It feels organized. They’re trying to make people think less of me, push me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws have been deliberately conspiring or planning against you in a way that goes beyond normal family conflict or disagreement?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, that’s what I’m saying. It’s not just normal arguing, not like when families bicker over food or who didn’t visit who. I came in because I knew my in-laws were doing more than that—using the altar, the parish committee, all those church people—to push me out.\n\nAnd it’s like they want everyone to forget what I’ve done for this family. I’ve spent years doing things, showing up, helping, and now suddenly it’s like I’m being erased. They act like it’s nothing, but I can see the planning in it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me exactly who you believe is involved in this effort to push you out and make people think less of you?\n\nPatient: My in-laws, mostly. I mean, that’s where it starts. And then the parish committee people, the ones connected with the altar and the church things—they may smile and act respectful, but they’re part of it too, or at least they’re letting themselves be used.\n\nIt’s hard to name every person because they don’t come out and say it plainly, you know? It’s in how they arrange things, who they thank, who they leave out. My sister-in-law especially has always been good at that—making herself look proper while I’m made to look like nothing. And now with the church involved, it feels like they’re trying to push me out completely, like all I’ve done for the family should just be forgotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these people you mentioned are coordinating their actions in a planned or organized way, or do you think they’re just acting on their own but in a way that ends up working against you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s planned. I mean, maybe not everyone sits in one room and says it out loud like that, but it’s too organized to be just accidents. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that church respect, to push me out.\n\nAnd you know, in families people always remember who brought what dish or who helped with the children, but suddenly with me it’s like none of it happened. All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. Someone is making it happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of something your in-laws or the parish committee did that made you feel like they were trying to erase you or make others forget what you've done for the family?\n\nPatient: One example is at the church, with the altar and the parish committee. They act like it’s just church business, you know, flowers here, names there, who gets thanked, who stands where—but it’s not just that. I could see how they were using it to push me out.\n\nAnd my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. They got those church people involved, or let them be involved, so everyone would stop remembering what I’ve done for this family. All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.\n\nIt’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.\n\nBut it feels coordinated. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nAnd you know, families can be petty—my aunt once stopped speaking to someone over a missing casserole dish for years—but this isn’t that. And now with the church involved, it feels like they’re trying to push me out completely, like all I’ve done for the family should just be forgotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these people you mentioned are coordinating their actions in a planned or organized way, or do you think they’re just acting on their own but in a way that ends up working against you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s planned. I mean, maybe not everyone sits in one room and says it out loud like that, but it’s too organized to be just accidents. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that church respect, to push me out.\n\nAnd you know, in families people always remember who brought what dish or who helped with the children, but suddenly with me it’s like none of it happened. All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. Someone is making it happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of something your in-laws or the parish committee did that made you feel like they were trying to erase you or make others forget what you've done for the family?\n\nPatient: One example is at the church, with the altar and the parish committee. They act like it’s just church business, you know, flowers here, names there, who gets thanked, who stands where—but it’s not just that. I could see how they were using it to push me out.\n\nAnd my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. They got those church people involved, or let them be involved, so everyone would stop remembering what I’ve done for this family. All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.\n\nIt’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.\n\nBut it feels coordinated. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. No, I don’t think it’s coincidence. Maybe one little thing could be coincidence, like someone forgetting to put your name on a list—my cousin did that once for a wedding and there was trouble for months—but not this, not all of it together.

I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.\n\nBut it feels coordinated. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. Whether they sat in a room and planned it or just all know what they’re doing without saying it straight out… the result is the same. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nAnd you know, families can be petty—my aunt once stopped speaking to someone over a missing casserole dish for years—but this isn’t that. This has the church wrapped around it, so it looks official, like I’m the one who doesn’t belong. Whether it’s some big organized thing or just them all lining up together, it feels like an effort to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe the actions of your in-laws and the parish committee are being done with specific intent to harm you, or if you think it might be more of a coincidence that they are all acting this way toward you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s coincidence. Maybe one little thing could be coincidence, like someone forgetting to put your name on a list—my cousin did that once for a wedding and there was trouble for months—but not this, not all of it together.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That feels intentional to me. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Some people notice, I think. They don’t always say it straight out, because in church everybody wants to smile and pass the bulletin like nothing’s wrong. But you can tell. A look, a pause, someone suddenly busy when I come near.

I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just having another family argument with me. They were using the altar and that parish committee to push me out, to make people forget what I’ve done for this family. And that kind of thing… well, people see more than they admit.

But then again, maybe I’m the only one willing to say it. My sister used to say, “Don’t make waves,” even when the roof was leaking right over the dining table. People will let a whole ceiling fall in before they’ll admit there’s a problem. So yes, I think others notice, but they keep quiet.

It’s in how they arrange things, who they thank, who they leave out. My sister-in-law especially has always been good at that—making herself look proper while I’m made to look like nothing. And now with the church involved, it feels like they’re trying to push me out completely, like all I’ve done for the family should just be forgotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these people you mentioned are coordinating their actions in a planned or organized way, or do you think they’re just acting on their own but in a way that ends up working against you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s planned. I mean, maybe not everyone sits in one room and says it out loud like that, but it’s too organized to be just accidents. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that church respect, to push me out.\n\nAnd you know, in families people always remember who brought what dish or who helped with the children, but suddenly with me it’s like none of it happened. All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. Someone is making it happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of something your in-laws or the parish committee did that made you feel like they were trying to erase you or make others forget what you've done for the family?\n\nPatient: One example is at the church, with the altar and the parish committee. I could see how they were using it to push me out.\n\nAnd my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I don’t know about everywhere else… I mean, mostly it’s there, with my in-laws and the church. That’s where I can see it plain as day. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. And now with the church involved, it feels like they’re trying to push me out completely, like all I’ve done for the family should just be forgotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that these people you mentioned are coordinating their actions in a planned or organized way, or do you think they’re just acting on their own but in a way that ends up working against you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s planned. I mean, maybe not everyone sits in one room and says it out loud like that, but it’s too organized to be just accidents. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that church respect, to push me out.\n\nAnd you know, in families people always remember who brought what dish or who helped with the children, but suddenly with me it’s like none of it happened. All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. Someone is making it happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of something your in-laws or the parish committee did that made you feel like they were trying to erase you or make others forget what you've done for the family?\n\nPatient: One example is at the church, with the altar and the parish committee. They act like it’s just church business, you know, flowers here, names there, who gets thanked, who stands where—but it’s not just that. I could see how they were using it to push me out.\n\nAnd my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. They got those church people involved, or let them be involved, so everyone would stop remembering what I’ve done for this family. All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.\n\nIt’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.\n\nBut it feels coordinated. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. Whether they sat in a room and planned it or just all know what they’re doing without saying it straight out… the result is the same. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nAnd you know, families can be petty—my aunt once stopped speaking to someone over a missing casserole dish for years—but this isn’t that. This has the church wrapped around it, so it looks official, like I’m the one who doesn’t belong. Whether it’s some big organized thing or just them all lining up together, it feels like an effort to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe the actions of your in-laws and the parish committee are being done with specific intent to harm you, or if you think it might be more of a coincidence that they are all acting this way toward you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s coincidence. Maybe one little thing could be coincidence, like someone forgetting to put your name on a list—my cousin did that once for a wedding and there was trouble for months—but not this, not all of it together.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That feels intentional to me. Maybe they wouldn’t say it out loud that way, but I know what it feels like when people are trying to make you disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe others in the church or family have noticed or acknowledged this effort to push you out, or do you feel like you’re the only one who sees it this way?\n\nPatient: Some people notice, I think. They don’t always say it straight out, because in church everybody wants to smile and pass the bulletin like nothing’s wrong. But you can tell. A look, a pause, someone suddenly busy when I come near.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just having another family argument with me. They were using the altar and that parish committee to push me out, to make people forget what I’ve done for this family. And that kind of thing… well, people see more than they admit.\n\nBut then again, maybe I’m the only one willing to say it. My sister used to say, “Don’t make waves,” even when the roof was leaking right over the dining table. People will let a whole ceiling fall in before they’ll admit there’s a problem. So yes, I think others notice, but they keep quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this effort to push you out and make people forget your contributions is happening in other areas of your life, outside of your family and the church?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about everywhere else… I mean, mostly it’s there, with my in-laws and the church. I came in because it wasn’t just arguing anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nOutside of that… well, people talk, don’t they? I don’t know if I’d say coworkers, because I’m not really around that kind of thing now. Friends… well, friends hear things, don’t they? I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore—they were using the altar, the parish committee, all of that church respect, to push me out.\n\nAnd you know, in families people always remember who brought what dish or who helped with the children, but suddenly with me it’s like none of it happened. All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. Someone is making it happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of something your in-laws or the parish committee did that made you feel like they were trying to erase you or make others forget what you've done for the family?\n\nPatient: One example is at the church, with the altar and the parish committee. They act like it’s just church business, you know, flowers here, names there, who gets thanked, who stands where—but it’s not just that. I could see how they were using it to push me out.\n\nAnd my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. They got those church people involved, or let them be involved, so everyone would stop remembering what I’ve done for this family. All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.\n\nIt’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.\n\nBut it feels coordinated. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. Whether they sat in a room and planned it or just all know what they’re doing without saying it straight out… the result is the same. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nAnd you know, families can be petty—my aunt once stopped speaking to someone over a missing casserole dish for years—but this isn’t that. This has the church wrapped around it, so it looks official, like I’m the one who doesn’t belong. Whether it’s some big organized thing or just them all lining up together, it feels like an effort to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe the actions of your in-laws and the parish committee are being done with specific intent to harm you, or if you think it might be more of a coincidence that they are all acting this way toward you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s coincidence. Maybe one little thing could be coincidence, like someone forgetting to put your name on a list—my cousin did that once for a wedding and there was trouble for months—but not this, not all of it together.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That feels intentional to me. Maybe they wouldn’t say it out loud that way, but I know what it feels like when people are trying to make you disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe others in the church or family have noticed or acknowledged this effort to push you out, or do you feel like you’re the only one who sees it this way?\n\nPatient: Some people notice, I think. They don’t always say it straight out, because in church everybody wants to smile and pass the bulletin like nothing’s wrong. But you can tell. A look, a pause, someone suddenly busy when I come near.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just having another family argument with me. They were using the altar and that parish committee to push me out, to make people forget what I’ve done for this family. And that kind of thing… well, people see more than they admit.\n\nBut then again, maybe I’m the only one willing to say it. My sister used to say, “Don’t make waves,” even when the roof was leaking right over the dining table. People will let a whole ceiling fall in before they’ll admit there’s a problem. So yes, I think others notice, but they keep quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this effort to push you out and make people forget your contributions is happening in other areas of your life, outside of your family and the church?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about everywhere else… I mean, mostly it’s there, with my in-laws and the church. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s organized, yes. Maybe they wouldn’t call it a “plan,” because people like that never write it down on paper like a shopping list. But I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.

And you know, it’s funny, my mother used to say if two people always show up with the same story, somebody rehearsed it first. Like at Thanksgiving when everybody suddenly knew not to ask about the gravy boat I bought—anyway, that’s not the point. The point is, it feels deliberate. Too many things line up for it to be an accident.

All the things I did for this family, they want everyone to forget. That doesn’t happen by itself. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. Whether they sat in a room and planned it or just all know what they’re doing without saying it straight out… the result is the same. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. This has the church wrapped around it, so it looks official, like I’m the one who doesn’t belong. I could see how they were using it to push me out.\n\nAnd my in-laws, they weren’t only arguing with me at home or saying rude things. They got those church people involved, or let them be involved, so everyone would stop remembering what I’ve done for this family. All the years I helped, showed up, did things no one else wanted to do—suddenly I’m not mentioned, like I never mattered.\n\nIt’s like when you clean the whole house and then someone else lights one candle and everyone praises them. That happened to my cousin once at a wedding, actually, with the seating and all that—but anyway, with me, it was the altar, the parish committee, and my in-laws behind it. That’s when I knew it was more than normal family conflict.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific action taken by your in-laws or the parish committee that you felt was directly aimed at making others forget your contributions?\n\nPatient: The specific thing was the way they used the altar and the parish committee, like I said. It wasn’t just, “Oh, we forgot to mention her,” no. My in-laws were behind it, or at least pushing it along, and then suddenly the church people are arranging things so I’m left out, not thanked, not seen.\n\nI came in because I knew it wasn’t just arguing anymore. Families argue, everybody argues—my mother used to say if nobody argues at a holiday then someone is hiding something—but this was different. They were using the altar, the parish committee, that whole respectable church setting, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That’s what hurt me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are actively working together to damage your reputation or exclude you from the family and church community, rather than just overlooking or forgetting your contributions?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I don’t think it’s just forgetting. People forget little things, like who brought the salad to a picnic—my sister used to forget that every single year—but this is bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, the parish committee, all that church respectability, to push me out. And when you do it through the church, people believe it. They think, “Oh, it must be proper, it must be decided,” and then suddenly I’m not seen, not remembered.\n\nSo yes, I think they’re working together, or at least moving in the same direction, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee have the ability to control how people remember or forget things about you, or influence others’ thoughts about you through the church?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t know if they can control thoughts like that exactly, but through the church, people listen. They see the altar, they hear what the parish committee says, who gets named, who gets left out, and then they start remembering things the way those people want them to.\n\nThat’s why I came in, because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. And once church people act like you don’t matter, others follow along. It’s not like a regular family disagreement over dinner, you know—though my brother-in-law once made a whole scene over who carved the turkey—but this is different. This feels planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that specific people in the parish committee have communicated with your in-laws to exclude you, or do you think they’re acting independently but their actions still seem coordinated to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can name who called who, if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting in their kitchens listening to phone calls—though my mother-in-law always did love a phone call, she could stretch one conversation from supper to bedtime.\n\nBut it feels coordinated. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar, and the parish committee, to push me out and make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. Whether they sat in a room and planned it or just all know what they’re doing without saying it straight out… the result is the same. I’m left out, and they look respectable while doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your in-laws and the parish committee are part of a larger, organized effort to harm your reputation, or do you think their actions are more about personal disagreements that have escalated?\n\nPatient: I think it started as family things, maybe, but it’s gone past that. I came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family.\n\nAnd you know, families can be petty—my aunt once stopped speaking to someone over a missing casserole dish for years—but this isn’t that. This has the church wrapped around it, so it looks official, like I’m the one who doesn’t belong. Whether it’s some big organized thing or just them all lining up together, it feels like an effort to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe the actions of your in-laws and the parish committee are being done with specific intent to harm you, or if you think it might be more of a coincidence that they are all acting this way toward you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s coincidence. Maybe one little thing could be coincidence, like someone forgetting to put your name on a list—my cousin did that once for a wedding and there was trouble for months—but not this, not all of it together.\n\nI came in because I knew my in-laws weren’t just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out, to make everyone forget what I’ve done for this family. That feels intentional to me. Maybe they wouldn’t say it out loud that way, but I know what it feels like when people are trying to make you disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe others in the church or family have noticed or acknowledged this effort to push you out, or do you feel like you’re the only one who sees it this way?\n\nPatient: Some people notice, I think. Yes, that’s what I’m saying. It’s not just normal people not getting along, you know? Families argue, committees have their little opinions—my sister used to say church committees could fight over the color of a napkin if you let them.
